## T&E

### Summary
T&E refers to Threatened and Endangered Species requiring special conservation efforts. The panel recommended that fish hatcheries should prioritize T&E species recovery work, that the FWS should consider captive protection and experimental propagation of candidate species before official listing to ensure adequate numbers and genetic variability are available, and that hatchery staffing should include personnel specifically trained to evaluate the ecological and genetic performance of stocked fish used in recovery efforts.

## T-Test

### Summary
A T-Test is a statistical procedure that compares the means of two groups to judge whether the difference between them is larger than would be expected by chance. In this source, the tables report a t-statistic for each paired comparison, but the OCR damage makes the individual values unreliable for citation.

## Table 1
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
In fish science literature, "Table 1" typically refers to a foundational data table that presents diagnostic morphometric and meristic characters (measurements and counts of fin rays, scales, vertebrae, etc.) used to distinguish between fish taxa or populations. These tables serve as essential reference documents for species identification and taxonomic classification, often providing population-level summary statistics that researchers use to differentiate closely related species or evaluate intraspecific variation. The inclusion of Table 1 data is standard practice in ichthyological studies, enabling readers to verify taxonomic assignments and compare morphological characters across populations or sampling sites.

### Summary

## Tagging Effects

### Summary

## Tagging Studies

### Summary
Tagging studies involve marking individual fish to track them over time and collect repeated measurements. This study used tagging to obtain multiple length measurements from the same Rainbow Trout individuals, with data collected during 13 formal tagging occasions between 1993 and 2004, supplemented by angler-reported measurements.

## Tagging Study

### Summary
A mark-recapture approach in which fish are tagged with metal tags on the operculum, released, and later recaptured to measure growth and movement. ### Summary
The Tahoe Sucker (*Catostomus tahoe*) is a native cyprinid fish species endemic to the Lake Tahoe basin that serves as an important ecological indicator and forage species in Sierra Nevada aquatic ecosystems. In the context of endangered species management and fish facility operations, the Tahoe Sucker is relevant to recovery efforts for co-occurring threatened species like the cui-ui, as native sucker populations reflect broader watershed health and may be affected by fish passage operations and facility management at facilities like the Marble Bluff Fish Station. Understanding Tahoe Sucker population dynamics and habitat requirements is essential for developing comprehensive, multi-year management plans that protect both the sucker and dependent species within integrated recovery programs.

## Tapeats Creek
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tapeats Creek is a tributary of the Colorado River within the Grand Canyon that serves as an important sampling locality in ichthyological surveys and a critical habitat for both native and introduced fish species. The creek's cooler waters support populations of native species such as speckled dace (Rhinichthys osculus) and introduced rainbow trout (Salmo gairdneri), making it a key site for monitoring fish communities and assessing the ecological impacts of species introductions in the Grand Canyon ecosystem. Its inclusion in multiple regional fish surveys underscores its significance for understanding tributary-mainstem fish habitat associations and conservation status in this unique aquatic system.

### Summary

## Taxonomy
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Taxonomy in fish science refers to the systematic classification and organization of fish species based on their evolutionary relationships, morphological characteristics, and genetic similarities, providing a hierarchical framework for identifying and naming aquatic organisms. This foundational discipline underpins conservation biology and aquatic ecology by enabling researchers to assess biodiversity, track population dynamics, and establish priorities for species protection. Effective taxonomic systems also support the management and retrieval of comparative biological data across fish taxa, facilitating interdisciplinary research and informed fisheries management decisions.

### Context

## Teays River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Teays River is a significant paleogeographic and biogeographic feature that refers to an ancient river system that once flowed through the central United States before being disrupted by glaciation during the Pleistocene epoch. In fish science and conservation biology, the Teays River system is important for understanding historical drainage patterns and fish distribution, as it helps explain the current geographic separation and genetic differentiation of fish populations across modern river systems like the Missouri and Mississippi rivers. The concept is used to interpret phylogeographic patterns and population structure in stream-dwelling fishes, where populations that were historically connected through the Teays drainage system became isolated as the ancient river was diverted by advancing glaciers, resulting in the distinct upland-stream adapted populations observed today.

### Context

## Tecopa Pupfish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Tecopa Pupfish (*Cyprinodon nevadensis calidae*) is a spring-endemic subspecies from the Ash Meadows/Death Valley region of Nevada-California that is likely extinct or extremely rare in its native habitat, with no confirmed sightings in recent surveys despite occasional unconfirmed local reports. As a representative of the highly specialized and geographically isolated pupfish fauna of the Great Basin thermal springs, it exemplifies the conservation challenges facing desert aquatic endemics threatened by habitat alteration from geothermal development and other anthropogenic disturbances. Its presumed loss underscores the critical importance of establishing refuge populations and conducting targeted surveys for surviving populations of these irreplaceable spring-endemic fishes.

## Teleost Fish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Teleost fish represent the largest and most diverse infraclass of bony fishes (Osteichthyes), characterized by a specialized swim bladder, homocercal tail fin, and highly derived skeletal and muscular systems that have enabled their ecological success across virtually all aquatic environments. As the dominant vertebrate group in most freshwater and marine ecosystems, teleosts are central subjects of study in ichthyology, conservation biology, and aquatic ecology due to their tremendous species diversity, economic importance to fisheries, and role as sentinel organisms for assessing ecosystem health. Their osmoregulatory capabilities and physiological plasticity make them particularly valuable model organisms for understanding how fish adapt to and maintain homeostasis in varied aquatic conditions.

## Tellico Dam
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Tellico Dam is a hydroelectric dam project on the Tellico River in Tennessee that became a landmark case in conservation biology and fisheries science due to its conflict with the protection of the snail darter (*Percina tanasi*), an endangered endemic fish species. The dam's construction threatened the river habitat critical for the snail darter's survival, making it a pivotal example of how infrastructure development intersects with endangered species protection under the Endangered Species Act. This case fundamentally shaped discussions about balancing economic development with aquatic ecosystem conservation and species preservation in North America.

## Temperature Preference
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Temperature preference in fish refers to the range of water temperatures that an individual species actively selects and maintains when given a choice among available thermal conditions, reflecting the fish's physiological optimum and ecological niche requirements. This concept is fundamental to understanding fish behavior, distribution, and vulnerability to environmental stressors such as thermal pollution from industrial sources like power plant effluents. Temperature preference data is typically measured using standardized laboratory apparatus with thermal gradients and is essential for conservation planning, assessing impacts of climate change, and predicting how fish populations will respond to altered thermal regimes in aquatic ecosystems.

### Context

## Temperature-Dependent Behavior

### Summary
Temperature-dependent behavior describes how fish activity and habitat use respond to thermal conditions. This source found that monthly Razorback Sucker activity increased significantly with mean monthly river temperature (P < 0.001; R² = 0.59) but showed no relationship with river discharge, suggesting temperature alone can drive movements and that fish used deeper water during summer months when temperatures peaked at 26.2°C, possibly tracking optimal temperatures.

## Tenkiller Reservoir
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tenkiller Reservoir is an Oklahoma impoundment notable in fisheries science as a dimictic lake that develops strong summer thermal stratification with severe dissolved oxygen depletion in its hypolimnion, conditions that significantly influenced downstream fishery management when hydroelectric discharge released anoxic water into the Illinois River tailwater below the dam. The reservoir's limnological characteristics, particularly the cold, oxygen-poor hypolimnetic outflows, became a focal point for mid-20th century research examining whether tailwater conditions could support coldwater fish species like rainbow trout through put-and-take fishery approaches and what impacts reservoir operations had on downstream aquatic communities.

The Tennessee River is a major freshwater drainage system in the southeastern United States that has served as an important study area for ichthyological research and fish conservation efforts. As a significant tributary of the Ohio River, it supports diverse cyprinid and other fish communities that have been subjects of systematic taxonomic and ecological investigations. ## Tennessee Valley Authority
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Tennessee Valley Authority (TVA) is a major federal agency responsible for managing hydroelectric dams, water resources, and fisheries programs across the Tennessee River basin and southeastern United States, playing a critical role in both aquatic habitat alteration and fish conservation efforts. As a large-scale water management system, TVA operations have significantly impacted riverine ecosystems through dam construction and water regulation, while simultaneously establishing hatchery facilities and restoration programs to mitigate species declines and propagate both native and anadromous fishes for population recovery. TVA's dual role—as both a driver of habitat modification and an institution actively engaged in fisheries restoration—makes it central to understanding modern freshwater conservation challenges and management strategies in the southeastern United States.

## Terrapene Coahuila
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Terrapene coahuila, commonly known as the Coahuila box turtle or "Bisagrita," is a highly endemic and critically endangered aquatic turtle species found exclusively in the Cuatrociénegas Basin of Mexico, representing the only fully aquatic member of the predominantly terrestrial Terrapene genus. This species is of significant conservation concern due to its extremely restricted geographic range (approximately 1,500 km²) and its dependence on isolated spring-fed aquatic ecosystems that are vulnerable to environmental degradation and water resource depletion. Its presence in the Cuatrociénegas Basin, which harbors disproportionately high levels of endemic aquatic fauna, makes it a key indicator species for assessing the ecological health and conservation status of this unique inland aquatic ecosystem.

## Territorial Behavior In Fish
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Territorial behavior in fish refers to the aggressive defense and active maintenance of specific aquatic spaces by individual fish or groups, typically to secure access to resources such as food, breeding sites, or shelter. This behavior is particularly pronounced during reproductive periods and involves ritualized displays, combat, or exclusion of conspecifics from defined areas. Understanding territorial behavior is essential for fisheries management and conservation, as it influences population dynamics, breeding success, and spatial distribution patterns within aquatic ecosystems.

## Territoriality
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Territoriality in fish refers to the behavior by which individual fish or groups defend and maintain exclusive access to specific areas of aquatic habitat, including resources such as food, shelter, and breeding sites. This behavior is fundamental to understanding population dynamics and spatial distribution in fish communities, as territorial establishment influences habitat use patterns, intraspecific competition, and ultimately population density and structure. The degree of territoriality varies among species and environmental conditions, with factors such as resource availability, population density, and sex ratios affecting the expression and intensity of territorial defense behaviors.

### Context
> - **Food resource base:** desert succulents (agave, yucca, sotol, prickly pear) and leguminous trees/shrubs (mesquite, huizache, huizachillo), each maturing at different times/locations, incentivizing high mobility. - **Water resource base:** virtually no running water in the region; water restri...
> -- *[[TetheredNomadismAndWaterTerrit_13512]]*

## The Arizona Nature Conservancy

### Summary
The Arizona Nature Conservancy is a conservation organization that, as of the 1980s, played a central role in documenting and addressing the crisis facing Arizona's native freshwater fish fauna, which had declined from 31 pre-settlement species to a state where one was extinct and four were extirpated. The organization developed and implemented conservation strategies focused on habitat preservation and maintenance of stable stream flows to combat the primary threats to these species: exotic fish introductions and loss of perennial aquatic ecosystems. Through projects such as lake and stream renovations, the Arizona Nature Conservancy worked to restore native fish populations and protect remaining populations in Arizona's freshwater systems.

## Theodore Nicholas Gill

### Summary
Theodore Nicholas Gill (1837-1914) published over 500 papers primarily on systematic ichthyology, focusing on "arrangement of fishes in classes, orders and families, yielding a more natural and restricted distribution of genera." ## Thermal Acclimation
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Thermal acclimation refers to the physiological and biochemical adjustments that fish undergo in response to sustained exposure to different water temperatures, allowing them to maintain metabolic performance and survival across varying thermal environments. This process involves changes in enzyme activity, membrane composition, and other cellular mechanisms that enable fish to adapt their temperature tolerance limits and optimize their metabolic efficiency to match the ambient conditions they experience seasonally or chronically. Understanding thermal acclimation is critical for fisheries management and conservation, particularly in assessing how fish populations respond to environmental temperature fluctuations and their capacity to tolerate changing aquatic conditions.

### Context

## Thermal Pollution
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Thermal pollution refers to the degradation of aquatic ecosystems caused by the discharge of heated water from industrial sources, particularly power plants and other industrial facilities, which elevates water temperatures beyond the tolerance ranges of native fish species and disrupts their physiological processes. This form of water quality degradation alters thermal gradients that fish depend on for migration, reproduction, and metabolic regulation, and often compounds other stressors such as dissolved oxygen depletion, making it a significant concern in fisheries conservation and aquatic ecosystem management. Thermal pollution is typically assessed using standardized laboratory methods that simulate realistic thermal conditions to understand fish behavioral responses and establish water quality criteria for the protection of aquatic life.

### Context
> This paper is **not** a fish-centric or taxon-centric study; rather, it is a physical/hydrological investigation of heat exchange and water temperature prediction in a thermally loaded stream reach affected by cooling-water discharge from a coal-fired power plant. ## Thermal Stratification
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Thermal stratification is the formation of distinct horizontal layers of water with different temperatures in lakes and reservoirs, typically occurring during summer months when surface water warms while deeper water remains cold. This density-based separation significantly impacts fish distribution, metabolism, and survival, as many species are restricted to specific temperature zones and may experience stress or mortality if trapped in unsuitable layers, particularly in hypoxic deeper waters during extended stratification periods. Understanding thermal stratification is essential for fisheries management and conservation, as it influences fish habitat quality, spawning success, and seasonal population dynamics.

### Context

## Thermal Tolerance
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Thermal tolerance refers to the range of water temperatures within which a fish species can survive and maintain essential physiological functions, encompassing both the upper and lower lethal temperature limits as well as the temperatures at which optimal performance occurs. This concept is fundamental to understanding fish ecology and conservation, as it determines species distribution, habitat suitability, and vulnerability to environmental changes such as climate warming and thermal pollution. Thermal tolerance varies among species and can be influenced by factors such as acclimation history, life stage, and evolutionary adaptation to specific thermal environments.

## Thermocline
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
A thermocline is a distinct layer in a stratified water body where temperature changes rapidly with depth, typically separating warmer surface waters from cooler deeper waters. In fisheries and aquatic ecology, the thermocline is significant because it often coincides with sharp changes in dissolved oxygen and other chemical conditions that profoundly affect fish distribution, behavior, and survival. Understanding thermocline dynamics is essential for fisheries management, particularly in reservoirs and lakes where thermal stratification can create inhospitable conditions in deeper waters and influence the downstream thermal and chemical composition of discharged water.

The Thicktail Chub (*Gila crassicauda*) is a now-presumed-extinct California minnow native to the San Francisco Bay region and Central Valley drainages that was formally redescribed by Robert Rush Miller in 1963 based on museum specimens. This cyprinid species experienced severe range contraction and habitat loss due to water diversion, dam construction, and introduced species competition, becoming one of North America's extinct fishes alongside other native California species like the Harelip Sucker. The Thicktail Chub serves as a significant case study in ichthyology and conservation biology, illustrating the broader decline of native fish fauna in California's aquatic ecosystems during the 20th century.

### Context
> - **24 total species** collected (12 native / 12 introduced) across 167 sites in Fresno, Tulare, Madera, Mariposa, Tuolumne, Stanislaus, and Calaveras counties. - **Range contraction in natives**: [[California Roach]], [[Hardhead]], [[Sacramento Squawfish]], and [[Sacramento Sucker]] show substan...
> -- *[[DeclineOfTheNativeFishFaunaOfT_1974]]*

> - **[[Harelip Sucker]]** (Catostomidae) — described as the "Passenger pigeon of the fish world"; the first fish confirmed extinct in the United States (last collected ~1893). - **[[Miller Lake Lamprey]]** (Petromyzontidae) — extinct following lake eradication of "trash fish" in Oregon (1950). - *...
> -- *[[EndangeredSpeciesTodayExtinctT_1977]]*

## Thomas Hill Reservoir

### Summary
Thomas Hill Reservoir is a 4,500-acre shallow cooling reservoir (mean depth <12 feet) in Missouri used for thermal discharge from a 475 megawatt fossil-fueled steam electric generating plant. The reservoir featured a 0.9-mile cooling canal that allowed heat dissipation before discharge, and during the 1967-68 winter fishery peak, an estimated 1,000 fish per day were harvested from less than 50 acres in the heated water arm where Largemouth Bass, White Crappie, and bullheads congregated and fed actively during winter.

### Context
> This 1970 conference paper presents research on the ecological effects of heated water discharges from fossil-fueled steam electric generating plants into cooling reservoirs in Missouri. Studies conducted at [[Montrose Lake]] (1500 acres) and [[Thomas Hill Reservoir]] (4500 acres) examined water...

### Summary
Threadfin Shad (*Dorosoma petenense*) is a small, planktivorous forage fish in the herring family (Clupeidae) that plays an important ecological role as a primary food source for predatory fish species in freshwater reservoirs and lakes throughout North America. Native to the southeastern United States but widely introduced into western reservoirs including those of the Colorado River Basin, threadfin shad support recreational and commercial fisheries by sustaining populations of larger gamefish such as largemouth bass and stripers. Their rapid reproduction, schooling behavior, and ability to thrive in warm-water impoundments make them a key species in fisheries management strategies, though their introduction into non-native ecosystems can have complex ecological consequences for native species assemblages.

### Summary
Threatened and Endangered Fish Species refers to fish populations classified under conservation status designations due to declining population numbers, habitat loss, or other ecological threats that place them at risk of extinction. These designations typically trigger protective legal frameworks and management interventions, such as captive breeding programs at fish hatcheries, diagnostic health assessments, and habitat restoration efforts, to prevent further population decline and promote species recovery. Understanding the health, genetic status, and ecological requirements of such species is critical for implementing effective conservation strategies in both wild and hatchery settings.

## Threatened And Endangered Species

### Summary
Threatened and Endangered Species (TES) in fish science refers to fish populations and communities that face significant risk of extinction or severe population decline due to habitat loss, overexploitation, or ecological disruption, and are formally designated under legislation such as the Endangered Species Act. These designations identify aquatic organisms requiring intensive conservation management and protection, including habitat preservation, population monitoring, and potentially captive breeding or hatchery support to prevent irreversible loss of species and genetic diversity. The conservation of TES represents a critical priority in fisheries management and aquatic ecology, as species extinctions are irreversible phenomena with cascading consequences for ecosystem integrity and biodiversity.

## Threatened Species
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
A threatened species in fish science refers to any fish population or taxon that is at risk of becoming endangered due to declining numbers, habitat loss, restricted range, or other conservation concerns, but which has not yet reached critically low populations requiring immediate emergency intervention. These species represent an intermediate conservation status requiring active management and monitoring to prevent further decline and potential extinction. In aquatic ecology and conservation biology, threatened species serve as important indicators of ecosystem health and are often prioritized for research, habitat restoration, and regulatory protection to ensure long-term population viability.

## Three-spined Stickleback
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The three-spined stickleback (Gasterosteus aculeatus) is a small freshwater and euryhaline fish species widely used as a model organism in ecological and evolutionary research due to its rapid adaptation to diverse environmental conditions and its sensitivity to physiological factors such as water temperature, photoperiod, and food availability. This species is particularly valuable in studies examining thyroid function and seasonal metabolic responses, as well as in conservation assessments of temperate aquatic ecosystems where it serves as an indicator of environmental quality and habitat integrity. Its small size, short generation time, and well-characterized behavioral and morphological traits make it an ideal subject for understanding how fish populations respond to cyclical environmental variables and seasonal changes in their aquatic habitats.

## Threespine Stickleback
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The threespine stickleback (Gasterosteus aculeatus) is a small, euryhaline fish species found across the Northern Hemisphere that serves as an important model organism in evolutionary biology and ecology due to its rapid adaptive radiation in isolated freshwater systems and well-documented phenotypic plasticity. In the context of conservation and fisheries science, threespine sticklebacks are significant as both indicators of aquatic ecosystem health and as subjects of concern regarding invasive species introductions, as populations introduced outside their native ranges can outcompete native fishes and disrupt freshwater communities. The species' presence in fossil records and contemporary ecosystems makes it valuable for understanding long-term changes in fish faunas and the impacts of environmental disturbance on native aquatic biodiversity.

Tiaroga cobitis, commonly known as the loach minnow, is a small cyprinid fish native to the southwestern United States that was recognized as a distinct genus separate from Rhinichthys based on morphological and systematic reassessment. This species is of significant conservation concern and has been the subject of multiple endangered species recovery planning efforts, reflecting its imperiled status in desert aquatic ecosystems of the American Southwest. The taxonomic reinstatement of Tiaroga as a genus distinct from other minnow groups represents an important refinement in the systematics of North American desert fishes and has implications for its conservation management and recovery strategies.

## Tiger Salamander
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Tiger Salamander (*Ambystoma tigrinum*) is an aquatic amphibian whose larval and neotenic forms are relevant to aquatic ecology and fisheries management, particularly as a predatory organism in freshwater pond ecosystems and as a commercially traded bait species in the aquatic trade industry. As apex predators in their larval stage, tiger salamanders are important subjects for studying food web dynamics and trophic interactions in shallow pond environments, while their commercial harvesting and sale as "waterdogs" for fishing bait represents a historically significant fisheries commodity in regions like Arizona. Conservation considerations for this species involve balancing its ecological role as a native predator with management of commercial harvest pressures and potential impacts on native fish populations.

## Tijuana Estuary

### Summary
The Tijuana Estuary is the receiving water body for the Tijuana River at the US-Mexico border that experiences significant pollutant loading from urban runoff and non-point sources. According to this proposal, the estuary receives anthropogenic pollutants including lead, cadmium, chromium, copper, nickel, and zinc, as well as conventional contaminants like nitrate, ammonia, phosphate, and suspended solids from the watershed. The research program planned to directly measure mass emissions of these contaminants into the estuary using automated sampling equipment during storm events and dry-weather conditions.

## Tijuana Natural Estuarine Research Reserve

### Summary
The Tijuana Natural Estuarine Research Reserve is a protected area that receives significant pollutant loading from urban runoff and non-point sources in the Tijuana River Watershed. ## Tijuana River Watershed

### Summary
The Tijuana River Watershed is a 4,500 square kilometer transborder basin spanning the US-Mexico border, with two-thirds located in Mexico. According to this 1998 research proposal, the watershed has experienced decades of raw sewage flows from Tijuana and Tecate, making it the single greatest source of lead and other anthropogenic pollutants to the coastal ocean of southern California and Baja California. The proposal outlines research to identify pollutant sources, measure contaminant loading to the estuary, and model relationships between land use patterns and water quality using GIS-based approaches.

## Tilapia
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tilapia refers to a genus of freshwater and brackish-water cichlid fishes native to Africa and the Middle East that have become widely introduced non-native species in aquatic systems worldwide, including the southwestern United States. In conservation and ecological contexts, tilapia are typically regarded as problematic invasive species due to their competitive and predatory impacts on native fish populations, particularly in isolated or vulnerable habitats such as desert springs and small ponds. Their successful establishment in non-native environments is attributed to their hardiness, rapid reproduction, and ability to thrive in degraded water conditions, making them a significant threat to endemic and endangered species in invaded ecosystems.

## Tilapia Aurea
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tilapia aurea, commonly known as the blue tilapia, is a cichlid species native to Africa that has become an invasive species in various aquatic ecosystems worldwide, including the southwestern United States. This generalist species is particularly problematic in simple, species-poor ecosystems like those found in the Chihuahuan Desert, where it can outcompete native fauna and fundamentally alter trophic structures through its omnivorous feeding habits and competitive dominance. Its establishment in artificial pond systems and canal networks, such as those at the ASU Research Park linked to the Salt River Project, exemplifies how non-native fish species can rapidly colonize and spread through human-modified aquatic infrastructure.

### Context

## Tilapia hornorum

### Summary
Tilapia hornorum is an African cichlid species characterized by 16-18 dorsal spines, 28-30 total dorsal spines plus rays, and 18-26 gill rakers, with breeding males displaying black coloration with red dorsal fin margins and silver-white patches. This source documents that T. hornorum was imported from the Tropical Fish Culture Institute in Malacca, Malaysia in 1962 and has largely hybridized with Tilapia mossambica to form a single reproducing complex in the Lower Colorado River Basin, with specimens showing T. hornorum characteristics most common along the mainstream Colorado River.

### Context
> This study documents the introduction, establishment, and taxonomic status of African cichlid fishes of the genus [[Tilapia]] in the [[Lower Colorado River Basin]] of the American Southwest. Between 1961 and 1982, multiple [[Tilapia]] species were imported and stocked throughout [[Arizona]] and...

## Tilapia Mossambica
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tilapia mossambica is a cichlid fish species widely utilized in aquaculture and ecological research studies, particularly valued for its rapid growth rates, hardiness in controlled pond environments, and use as a model organism for investigating fish productivity in relation to nutrient fertilization and trophic dynamics. This species has been employed in experimental investigations examining how varying nitrogen-phosphorus-potassium fertility treatments affect fish growth and ecosystem productivity in enclosed pond systems, making it an important subject in fisheries science for understanding aquaculture yield optimization and the response of fish populations to environmental conditions.

## Tilapia Nilotica
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tilapia nilotica, commonly known as the Nile tilapia, is a highly adaptable cichlid fish native to Africa that has become one of the most important aquaculture species globally due to its rapid growth rates, hardy nature, and efficient feed conversion. In ecological and conservation contexts, this species is frequently studied for its impacts as an introduced species in non-native aquatic ecosystems, where it can outcompete native fish populations and alter community structure through its omnivorous feeding habits and reproductive success. Its widespread use in comparative aquaculture studies and fisheries research makes it a model organism for understanding tilapiine biology, growth dynamics, and the ecological consequences of fish introductions in freshwater systems.

### Summary
Tilapia zillii, commonly known as the redbelly tilapia, is a cichlid species native to Africa that has been introduced into North American aquatic systems, including the lower Colorado River and artificial pond complexes in the southwestern United States. This omnivorous cichlid is of conservation concern as an invasive species that can compete with native fishes, alter aquatic vegetation through herbivory, and potentially hybridize with other introduced tilapiine fishes in colonized ecosystems. Like other tilapia species, T. zillii demonstrates significant ecological plasticity, allowing it to establish populations in diverse aquatic habitats from natural river systems to artificial impoundments and canal networks.

## Tims Ford Dam

### Summary
Tims Ford Dam created the impoundment on the upper Elk River and is responsible for thermal alteration of the downstream tailwaters, changing them from warm to cold water habitat. The dam's power generation operations cause water level fluctuations of 2-3 feet that significantly impact Boulder Darter reproductive habitat by altering the specific depth and current velocity conditions that males select for spawning sites.

## Tims Ford Reservoir

### Summary
Tims Ford Reservoir is an impoundment on the upper Elk River in Tennessee that has significantly impacted the Boulder Darter population. The reservoir has altered the thermal regime of the tailwaters below the dam from warm to cold water, and water level fluctuations of 2-3 feet caused by power generation operations at the reservoir create unstable habitat conditions that may strand nests or reduce reproductive success by altering the depth and velocity characteristics of nest cavities.

### Summary

## Tissue Transplantation

### Summary
Tissue transplantation is the grafting of tissue from one individual to another. The source uses it as a test: tissues from identical twins are interchangeable, so a successful graft suggests donor and host are essentially identical, while rejection signals genetic difference. Fish shipped alive to the Marquette aquarium were used for such experiments, and a fin graft from a black molly was rejected.

## Tombigbee River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Tombigbee River is a major aquatic system in northeastern Mississippi that serves as an important subject for ichthyological research and conservation, particularly notable for harboring a genetically unique, homozygous walleye population that exhibits distinct genetic characteristics compared to other North American walleye stocks. As a river system with extensive habitat diversity including major river channels, impoundments, and tributaries, the Tombigbee River represents a significant freshwater ecosystem for studying fish populations, genetic structure, and aquatic biodiversity across multiple habitat types. The river's ecological importance has made it a focal point for research examining both the genetic uniqueness of endemic fish populations and broader aquatic conservation issues related to riverine habitat management and species preservation.

Tonto National Forest represents a key geographic region within Arizona's Gila River drainage system that contains suitable native cold-water stream habitat for the reintroduction and conservation of federally endangered fish species, particularly the Gila Trout (*Oncorhynchus gilae*). As part of interagency conservation efforts in the 1990s, the forest's aquatic ecosystems were identified as historically important native range where endangered species could be reestablished to restore genetic diversity and population viability. The forest's lotic (flowing water) stream systems within the larger Gila River drainage provide the hydrological and thermal conditions necessary for cold-water native fish species recovery programs.

## Top Minnow

### Summary

## Topeka Shiner
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Topeka Shiner (*Notropis topeka*) is a small cyprinid fish historically distributed across clear-water tributary streams in the central Great Plains, particularly in Kansas, where it specialized in cool, clean-water habitats with moderate current. This species is notable for its ecological association with limestone spring-fed creeks and represents a component of Great Plains stream fish communities that indicate high water quality and relatively undisturbed aquatic ecosystems. The Topeka Shiner has become a conservation concern due to habitat degradation and range reduction, making it an important indicator species for assessing the ecological integrity of prairie stream systems.

## Total Population Manipulation

### Summary
A management approach in which all fish in a stream section are eradicated, typically with rotenone, and the section is then restocked with desired game species. ### Summary
The Totoaba is an endangered large fish species native to the Gulf of California and Colorado River Delta region that has become a focal point in conservation efforts for southwestern North American aquatic ecosystems. Historically found in estuarine and freshwater habitats at the mouth of the Colorado River, the species has experienced severe population declines due to habitat loss, water management practices, and other anthropogenic pressures. ## Totten Reservoir

### Summary
Totten Reservoir is one of nine Colorado waters with approved lake management plans since inception of the Recovery Program that are exempted from further review under these interim procedures. ## Toxaphene
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Toxaphene is a chlorinated camphene-based insecticide that was employed as a piscicide (fish poison) in mid-20th century fisheries management to selectively eliminate undesirable fish species such as carp and yellow perch from aquatic ecosystems. Developed and tested by fisheries agencies like the Arizona Game and Fish Commission in the 1950s, toxaphene was applied experimentally as a population control method to manage rough fish and improve conditions for more desirable game fish species. Though effective as a fish toxicant, toxaphene's use in aquatic systems raised environmental and ecological concerns that eventually led to restrictions and phase-out of this and similar chemical piscicides in modern conservation practice.

## Trammel Nets
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Trammel nets are passive fishing gear consisting of multiple layers of netting with different mesh sizes that entangle fish as they attempt to pass through, making them particularly useful for sampling in specific aquatic habitats where active gear like electrofishing may be ineffective or inappropriate. In fisheries science and conservation work, trammel nets are strategically deployed to capture fish populations while minimizing harm compared to some alternative methods, though they require careful monitoring and standardized protocols to ensure consistent and representative sampling. These nets are especially valuable for surveying fish communities in larger water bodies, complex habitats, and situations where electrical or mechanical sampling methods present logistical or ecological constraints.

### Context

## Trash Catcher Dam

### Summary
A Trash Catcher Dam is a low-profile habitat improvement structure (maximum 20 inches high) designed to create pools and slow water flow in shallow, unproductive stream sections. The dam consists of steel fence posts spaced about 2 feet apart supporting heavy-duty hogwire fencing on the upstream side, with boulders placed on the upstream apron for stability and natural stream debris (leaves, twigs, aquatic plants) accumulating to impound water. The structure must have a level top across the channel with ends extending into both banks to prevent erosion, and functions best when positioned to deepen holes around natural features like stumps and boulders in shaded areas.

### Context

These low-profile dams (maximum 20 inches high) use steel posts, hogwire fencing, rocks, and accumulated stream debris to create...

## Travertine
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Travertine in aquatic ecology refers to porous, calcium carbonate mineral deposits that accumulate around thermal springs and freshwater habitats, creating structurally complex substrates that support specialized microbial and invertebrate communities. These deposits provide essential microhabitats for adapted organisms such as scraper snails and interstitial crustaceans, which exploit the hard surfaces for algal grazing and the crevices and tube-like channels for refuge and foraging in thermally unique ecosystems. Travertine formations are particularly significant in desert spring systems where they function as the primary structural habitat supporting endemic and thermophilic fauna adapted to these geographically isolated environments.

### Context

## Treaty of 1944

### Summary

### Summary

## Trichodina
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Trichodina is a ciliate protozoan parasite that infests the gills of fish, ranging from light to heavy infections depending on environmental and host conditions. As a common gill parasite in aquatic systems, Trichodina infections are frequently documented in fish disease surveys and can indicate poor water quality or stressed host populations, though it is often observed as a non-causative agent alongside other pathogens rather than as a primary mortality factor. Understanding Trichodina prevalence is important in fish conservation and aquaculture management, particularly for sensitive species like the Gila topminnow that inhabit specialized habitats with limited natural disease exposure.

### Summary
Trichomycteridae is a family of small catfishes found primarily in South American freshwater systems, particularly in Andean and lowland river basins, characterized by their diminutive size and often cryptic benthic habits. Members of this family are of interest to ichthyologists and conservation biologists due to their diversity in geographically isolated river systems, their roles in aquatic food webs, and their sensitivity to habitat degradation and water pollution. The family's ecological and taxonomic importance has made it a subject of systematic study and species description efforts throughout the twentieth century.

Trichoptera, commonly known as caddisflies, are an order of freshwater aquatic insects whose larval and pupal stages are crucial components of benthic macroinvertebrate communities in streams and rivers. ## Trophic Cascade
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
A trophic cascade is a series of ecological changes triggered by the addition or removal of a top predator or key species in an aquatic food web, where alterations at one trophic level propagate through lower levels and fundamentally restructure community composition and ecosystem function. In fish ecology and conservation contexts, trophic cascades are particularly significant when invasive fish species or changes in predator populations (such as alewife populations behind barriers) alter the abundance of intermediate predators and prey species, ultimately affecting primary productivity and water quality. Understanding trophic cascades is essential for predicting the ecosystem-wide consequences of species invasions, barrier removal, and predator management in freshwater and marine systems.

### Context
> - **Colonization success rates** are low: estimates range from ~10% (Williamson) up to a likely-overestimated 35%; paleobiological estimates (Vermeij) for Miocene–Pleistocene biotic exchanges show most taxa achieving <1–24% establishment, rarely up to 46%. - **Impact prevalence**: Only an estimat...
> -- *[[BiologicalInvasionsLessonsForE_16179]]*

## Trout
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Trout are a group of freshwater and anadromous fish species (including Brown, Rainbow, and Eastern Brook trout) that occupy significant ecological and economic roles in aquatic systems, serving as important indicators of water quality and primary targets for fisheries management and restocking programs. In scientific practice, trout are characterized by specific morphological features used in population monitoring, such as standardized scale collection sites on the body, and their reproductive biology is studied to understand fecundity patterns and life history strategies across different habitat types. Their presence in both freshwater streams and transitional estuarine environments makes them valuable model organisms for understanding habitat use, growth rates, and the effectiveness of artificial propagation efforts in conservation and fisheries science.

### Summary
Trout Creek refers to a small montane stream system, exemplified by a third-order stream in central Colorado, that serves as a model study site for investigating how environmental disturbances—particularly fluctuating water discharge and drought conditions—affect fish populations and aquatic macroinvertebrate communities. As a representative headwater ecosystem, Trout Creek provides important insights into stream ecology, fish habitat requirements, and the resilience of cold-water fish species to hydrological stress, making it a valuable reference system for understanding trout production dynamics and conservation needs in montane watersheds.

## Trout Fishery
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
A trout fishery is a managed aquatic system, typically in tailwater areas below dams or in cool-water environments, that supports populations of trout species through either natural reproduction or regular stocking for recreational and economic purposes. These fisheries are often dependent on specific thermal and water quality conditions, such as cold hypolimnetic discharge from impoundments, which create suitable habitat for coldwater species while potentially altering native warmwater fish communities. Trout fisheries represent an important trade-off in aquatic ecosystem management, providing significant recreational and economic value but sometimes at the expense of native species conservation and ecosystem integrity.

### Context
> **Thermal Regime Alteration:** - Hypolimnetic discharge reduced average maximum summer tailwater temperatures by as much as 6°C (from 25°C to 19°C in July) post-impoundment — a key driver of community change, enabling a put-and-take [[Trout Fishery]] but disadvantaging warmwater riverine species.
> -- *[[InfluenceOfAKentuckyFloodContr_15125]]*

> -- *[[LeesFerryTrophyTroutFishery_18301]]*

> -- *[[InfluenceOfAKentuckyFloodContr_15125]]*

## Trout Fishing

### Summary
The guide's trout section recommends small hooks (sizes 14 to 18) and light leaders, and prefers upstream fishing with nymphs or wet flies, with dry flies also fished upstream to avoid unnatural drag. It advises looking for trout in fast pools behind rocks and logs. For brown trout, which the authors call among the hardest trout to catch, artificial insects work best in summer, while worms do better in spring, fall and rainy periods.

## Trout Hatchery Pond Design

### Summary
This is the main subject of the 1960 study by Haskell, Davies and Reckahn, which examined how rectangular trout rearing ponds in New York State hatcheries should be built. The study tested circulation, aeration and screen area, and recommended uniform depth, straight sides, symmetrical full-width inlets and outlets, and adequate screening. The authors linked poor circulation to ammonia hot spots and uneven oxygen depletion, which they suggested could limit fish growth and health.

### Context
> This compiled document contains three unrelated pieces. The main fisheries content is a 1960 study of [[Trout Hatchery Pond Design]] in New York State, which tested [[Water Circulation]], [[Aeration Efficiency]] and [[Screen Area]] in rectangular rearing ponds. ## Trout Stocking
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Trout stocking is the deliberate introduction of hatchery-reared trout into natural or altered aquatic systems to supplement or establish populations for recreational fishing and conservation purposes. This management practice is particularly valuable in environments with degraded habitat, altered hydrology, or insufficient natural reproduction capacity, where wild populations cannot sustain themselves through spawning and recruitment alone. The efficacy and appropriate application of trout stocking programs depend on factors such as water quality, spawning habitat availability, and the specific ecological context of each system, making it a nuanced tool that complements rather than replaces wild trout conservation efforts.

### Context
> This Michigan Department of Conservation pamphlet (a revision of *For Better Fishing*, 1945) synthesizes multi-decade mark-recapture research conducted by the Institute for Fisheries Research to evaluate the efficacy of [[Trout Stocking]] programs across streams and lakes. ## Trout Unlimited
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Trout Unlimited is a conservation organization that advocates for the protection and restoration of wild trout populations and their aquatic habitats, working collaboratively with government agencies and resource managers on initiatives such as species reintroduction and wild trout management programs. The organization prioritizes the preservation of native trout species and the maintenance of healthy, minimally-impacted stream ecosystems that support natural reproduction, as exemplified by their involvement in Gila Trout reintroduction efforts in the American Southwest. Through local chapters and partnerships, Trout Unlimited bridges the interests of conservation biology, recreational fishing, and aquatic ecosystem management.

## Truckee River
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The Truckee River is a critical spawning tributary for the endangered cui-ui (*Chasmistes cujus*), an endemic lacustrine fish species that inhabits Pyramid Lake in Nevada and requires seasonal access to riverine habitat for reproduction. As the primary spawning migration corridor between the alkaline Pyramid Lake and suitable gravel spawning grounds, the lower Truckee River has become a focal point for cui-ui recovery efforts, particularly through fish passage structures like the Marble Bluff Fish Facility that enable upstream movement and compensate for historical habitat degradation and flow alterations. The river's hydrology, water quality, and fish passage are therefore essential management considerations for the recovery of this potamodromous species.

## Tubificidae
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tubificidae are a family of small, aquatic oligochaete worms (segmented worms) commonly found in freshwater sediments and benthic habitats, particularly in organically enriched or polluted environments. These organisms are ecologically significant as indicators of water quality and pollution, as they proliferate in low-oxygen conditions and heavily contaminated substrates, while also contributing substantially to sediment respiration and oxygen consumption in aquatic ecosystems. In ichthyology and fisheries contexts, Tubificidae are relevant as both potential food sources for fish species and as biological markers for assessing river and sediment health in polluted systems.

## Tule

### Summary
Tule (Scirpus americanus) is an emergent aquatic sedge plant that forms dense stands in shallow water. At Quitobaquito Springs, Tule provides critical habitat structure for juvenile Sonoran Mud Turtles, with trapping success 3.3-fold higher in Tule-vegetated areas, and the turtles also feed heavily on young Tule shoots when animal prey is scarce.

<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
The tullibee (Coregonus artedi) is a freshwater whitefish species native to North American lakes, particularly in the Great Lakes region and northern states like Minnesota and Michigan, where it inhabits oligotrophic (nutrient-poor) lakes as a commercially and recreationally important species. This coregonid occupies a pelagic to benthic ecological niche and has been extensively surveyed in fisheries population assessments using standardized sampling methods such as gillnetting and hoopnetting to monitor population dynamics and stock health. The tullibee serves as an important indicator species in aquatic ecology studies and fisheries management for understanding fish community structure and the ecological conditions of cold-water lake ecosystems.

### Summary
Turner and Tafanelli (1983) appears to be a foundational reference for understanding the habitat requirements and ecological characteristics of endemic cyprinid fishes in southwestern desert streams, particularly spiked dace and other threatened species of the Gila River basin. The work likely documents critical habitat associations with specific stream microhabitats such as boulder fields and aquatic vegetation, as well as the hydrological and geomorphological conditions necessary for persistence of these species in low-gradient, perennial desert streams. ## Twin Lakes, Colorado

### Summary
Twin Lakes are oligotrophic, cold-water mountain reservoirs in Colorado created by glacial action, consisting of an upper lake (263 hectares, 28 meters maximum depth) and lower lake (736.5 hectares, 27.1 meters maximum depth). The lakes support one of the best Lake Trout fisheries in the Western United States, with populations sustained primarily by Mysis relicta shrimp introduced in 1957, and were the primary focus of pre-operational ecological studies for the Mt. Elbert pumped-storage facility.

## Two-Source Capture-Recapture

### Summary
Two-source capture-recapture is a population estimation method that uses data from exactly two independent sampling occasions or sources to estimate total population size, including individuals missed by both sources. This source demonstrates applications to suicide data in Cambodia (158 observed, estimated 534-539 total) and heroin users in Thailand (1,447 observed, estimated 4,989-5,000 total). The method requires at least some overlap between sources (n₁₁ > 0) and performs best when sources are independent or show only moderate dependence (odds ratio < 3.5).

### Summary
Typhlichthys subterraneus, commonly known as the Southern Cavefish, is a relatively large-bodied troglobitic fish belonging to the family Amblyopsidae that inhabits cave systems primarily in the Ohio and Mississippi River basin region. This species is notable in conservation and ecological research as an example of a troglobite with an extensive geographic range despite its specialized cave habitat, demonstrating that range extent in subterranean fishes correlates more strongly with mobility and niche breadth than with body size alone. Its presence in multiple cave systems across the central United States makes it an important indicator species for understanding how aquatic organisms adapt to and persist in isolated subterranean environments.

Typhlomolge rathbuni (synonym of Eurycea rathbuni) is the Texas Blind Salamander, a subterranean species for which the artesian well site is the type-locality. It is one of several blind, depigmented organisms found in the same Edwards Aquifer habitat as Haideoporus texanus.

### Context
> This study describes the larva of *[[Haideoporus texanus]]*, the first hypogeous (subterranean) dytiscid beetle discovered in North America. Three larvae were collected from a flowing artesian well at the Aquatic Station (formerly [[San Marcos Federal Fish Hatchery]]) in [[San Marcos, Texas]]. The...
> -- *[[TheLarvaOfANewSubterraneanWate_16197]]*

## Tyus

### Summary

## Tyus 1974

### Summary
Tyus 1974 is a study whose results on alewife movement differ from this one. The Parker River study detected no night upstream movement, which contrasts with what Tyus 1974 and Richkus 1974 reported.

## Tyus Et Al. 1982
<!-- fm: auto_enriched: true -->

### Summary
Tyus et al. 1982 is a foundational ichthyological study that documented the reproductive failure and functional extirpation of wild Bonytail Chub populations in the Lower Colorado River Basin, establishing critical baseline data on the conservation status of this endemic fish species. This work provided essential evidence that remaining reservoir populations of Bonytail Chub were no longer reproducing in the wild, highlighting the severe ecological impacts of dam construction and habitat alteration on native Colorado River fishes. The study's findings became a cornerstone reference for understanding population decline mechanisms in endangered endemic fish species and informing recovery strategies for Colorado River basin conservation.

> -- *[[ControlOfNonnativeFishAtTheCon_1993_12_10]]*

> - **Historical habitat:** Upper Colorado River Basin mainstem/large river systems; specific native habitat and basic ecology are "virtually unknown" due to near-total loss of wild populations. - **Environmental degradation context:** [[Pearson 1957]] documented significant post-dam changes in **f...
> -- *[[DevelopmentOfABonytailReintrod_20256]]*

> - **Basin Endemism**: The Colorado River Basin exhibits 73% fish endemism, the highest of any river basin in the United States (Miller 1961), attributed to long isolation and extreme environmental conditions (fluctuating flow, temperature, turbidity). - **Species Guild Composition**: 13 native sp...
